  **Re-verified live immediately after, direct user report: "Another loop error jesus fucking christ were just wasting time."** Log (`logs/run_task_20260809_014104.log`) showed steps 43-48 stuck in a tight loop on 'Homeowner' — already checked earlier in the run — with the SAME "already checked — redirecting instead of blind Tab" guard firing every single step, never progressing, until the user interrupted at step 37 actionable (48 raw). Completion 10.2% (18/176 fields), wasted-step rate 24.3%.

  **Root cause, genuinely new and deeper than last fix: `ui_observer.py` never reads checkbox toggle state at all.** `value` is only ever populated from UIA's `ValuePattern` (or, as a fallback, `TextPattern`) — but checkboxes expose `TogglePattern`, not `ValuePattern`, so `ValuePattern` always silently returned nothing for them. Every checkbox in every form, checked or not, has therefore always reported `value=""`. This was invisible before tonight because checked-state was tracked separately (`self._checked_fields`, and a direct `BM_GETCHECK` read in the click guard) — nothing needed `navigation_protocol.find_visible_empty_target()`'s "is this field's value empty" check to be checkbox-aware until THIS session's redirect guards started routing through it. With that gap: right after 'Homeowner' got checked, `find_visible_empty_target()` kept seeing it as still-empty and kept returning it as its own "next target" — the redirect's own `_focus_element_via_uia("Homeowner")` call then `SetFocus`'d right back onto the very checkbox it was trying to move away from, which made the auto-fill fast path see 'Homeowner' as freshly focused again next step, re-entering the identical guard — a tight, self-reinforcing loop with no other moving part involved.

  **Fixed at the actual data source, not by patching the guard again.** `ui_observer.py` now falls back to `ctrl.GetPattern(TogglePattern)` and reads `.ToggleState` whenever `ValuePattern` left `value` empty, setting `value="checked"` for `ToggleState.On` — universal, applies to every checkbox in any form, not a Homeowner-specific patch. Also added a belt-and-suspenders fix in `agent.py`: both checkbox-check code paths (the TYPE-intercept merge branch and the click-guard's `BM_SETCHECK` success branch) now call `self._mark_attempted()` immediately after checking, so even a redirect computed within the SAME step — before the next observation cycle would reflect the new toggle state — can't offer the same checkbox back to itself.

  Regression tests: `tests/test_ui_observer_checkbox_toggle_state.py` (new, 6 tests — checked/unchecked toggle states read correctly, `ValuePattern` still wins when present so edit/combobox fields are untouched, missing pattern and read-failure both default safely to empty, and an end-to-end check that a correctly-valued checked checkbox is excluded from `find_visible_empty_target`). `tests/test_checkbox_no_blind_tab_after_check.py` extended with `TestMarkAttemptedPreventsSameStepSelfRedirectLoop` (1 test, proving the attempted-keys layer excludes a checkbox even while its value read is still stale). Full suite 412 passed / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures. Not yet re-verified live.

  **Re-verified live immediately after, direct user report: "We're in a loop and we're missing certain steps. I don't even know if we're fucking using the Transformer."** This run was actually one of the better ones so far (95 actionable steps, 39 fields filled, 22.2% completion, only 4.2% wasted-step rate) — but log evidence confirmed a real, separate bug, plus one non-bug worth answering directly.

  **Confirmed bug: the generic repeat-action guard was firing on genuine progress.** `logs/latest.log`, steps 108-113: the low-confidence-pointer fallback checked a genuinely DIFFERENT checkbox each step via `BM_SETCHECK` ('Roadside Assistance', 'GAP Insurance', 'Rideshare Coverage', 'New Car Replacement', ...), then reassigned `prediction` to a boilerplate "tab to move on" afterward — since the guard fingerprints the FINAL `prediction` right before execution (not what actually happened this step), three DIFFERENT successful checks in a row all ended with the IDENTICAL trailing tab, so it saw "same action 3x" and force-Tabbed AGAIN at step 111 through its own direct `self._executor.execute()` call — which, confirmed in the log, skips the normal `[OK] ExecutionResult` / `Validator` lines every other action gets. Real, distinguishable progress was happening every step; the guard just couldn't see past the leftover bookkeeping Tab. This also explains why the end-of-run per-step summary skips certain step numbers — any step whose result never went through the normal logged path (this guard's bypass included) isn't in that list, which is very plausibly what read as "missing steps."

  **Fixed with a per-step `_real_progress_this_step` flag**, set immediately after each of the two `BM_SETCHECK` real-check code paths (the TYPE-intercept merge branch and the click-guard's success branch). The repeat-action guard now skips its fingerprint check entirely — and clears history, since real progress legitimately breaks any actual stuck streak too — whenever that flag is set, letting the normal keyboard-tab path execute afterward with its usual logging. Generalizes beyond checkboxes: any future code path that performs a real state-changing action and then leaves a boilerplate "move on" prediction behind can set the same flag.

  **Answered directly, not guessed at: "are we using the Transformer."** Confirmed via log (142+ `[TRANSFORMER]` lines, one every raw step) and via `agent.py`'s own existing architecture comment (predates tonight, not something this session touched or broke): `_HIGH_CONF = 1.01  # effectively: never skip the LLM` is deliberate — the transformer's action-TYPE head is known to collapse to always one class, so it's never trusted to decide WHAT alone; the transformer's proven strength (click_acc ~0.76) is WHERE, so OPT2 always has the transformer supply the click position while the LLM supplies the value/action choice via `_merge`. So yes, the transformer runs and drives positioning on every single step — the `Transformer Dependency 50.5%` metric only counts steps where it acted with zero LLM involvement at all, which undercounts its real, constant involvement in this hybrid design. Flagged as a known, separate, PRE-EXISTING limitation (the collapsed action-type head) worth prioritizing separately if the team wants to close the >95% target — not something guessed at or touched in this fix.

  Regression tests: `tests/test_repeat_action_guard_ignores_real_progress.py` (new, 4 tests — three different real checkbox checks in a row never trigger the guard; real progress resets an in-progress streak instead of carrying over; the guard still catches a genuine stuck loop with no progress; three different predictions with no progress never false-triggers). Full suite 416 passed / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures. Not yet re-verified live.

  **Re-verified live immediately after, direct user report: "Jesus fucking christ another loop in the Drivers, why do you hate me?"** Log (`logs/latest.log`) showed a genuinely new, tight loop from 02:25:44 to past 02:27:17 (35+ repeats, ~1.5s apart): every cycle, the reclick-streak guard correctly detected the transformer's pointer stuck on already-filled 'Last Name', correctly redirected to Driver 3's 'Excluded Driver' cluster, correctly refocused to the shallowest remaining target 'First Name' — and then the VERY NEXT step showed the transformer's pointer already back on 'Last Name' again, as if the refocus never happened at all. Crucially, an earlier occurrence in the SAME run (line 1259) refocused successfully to 'Gender' and real progress followed — so the mechanism itself works; something field-specific broke for 'First Name'.

  **Root cause: `_find_uia_control_by_name` (the shared lookup behind `_focus_element_via_uia`/`_scroll_into_view_via_uia`) searches by accessible Name ALONE, with no way to tell apart the SAME bare label repeated across Driver 1/2/3** (no section prefix baked into the raw UIA Name property — the exact repeated-section collision class already fixed twice elsewhere this session: `state_validator.py`'s stable-key matching, and `agent.py`'s own `_attempt_key` rank disambiguation — just never carried into this UIA-level lookup, since until tonight's cluster-revealing mechanism started running on the Drivers tab, it was only ever used for uniquely-labeled fields). `EditControl(Name="First Name")` with no disambiguation always returns the FIRST match in the UIA tree — Driver 1's, already filled — regardless of which driver's cluster the caller actually meant. `SetFocus()` succeeded every single time (which is why no error ever surfaced), just on the WRONG element: the next step's auto-fill fast path saw an already-filled field focused, took the "navigate" branch instead of filling anything, the transformer's learned pointer (frozen near 'Last Name') re-triggered the identical "already-filled, redirect" guard, and around it went — forever, since nothing ever actually got typed into Driver 3's real, empty 'First Name'.

  **Fixed by disambiguating on position, not just name.** Every existing caller already has the specific target ELEMENT (with its own bbox, from `navigation_protocol`'s own scan) in hand before calling `_focus_element_via_uia` — they were just discarding that bbox and passing the bare label through. `_find_uia_control_by_name` gained an optional `expected_bbox` parameter: when given and more than one control shares the Name, it walks every match (via UIA's own `foundIndex`, 1-based) and returns whichever one's own `BoundingRectangle` center is closest to the expected position, instead of blindly trusting tree order. Defaults to the old first-match behavior when omitted, so nothing breaks for callers without a specific position. All 7 call sites across the file (`_focus_element_via_uia`/`_scroll_into_view_via_uia`, both redirect guards' primary AND shallow-refocus targets, the combobox-dropdown-fail redirect, the checkbox-already-checked redirect, and `_scroll_form_down`'s own ScrollIntoView call) now pass their already-known target bbox through.

  Regression tests: `tests/test_focus_element_via_uia.py` extended with `TestFindUiaControlByNameDisambiguatesRepeatedLabels` (4 new tests — picks the occurrence closest to the expected position among 3 same-labeled candidates; omitting `expected_bbox` keeps the old first-match behavior; the common single-occurrence case still works; an end-to-end check that `_focus_element_via_uia` actually threads the parameter through and calls `SetFocus` on the correct occurrence, not the wrong one). `tests/test_scroll_form_down_routing.py`'s existing ScrollIntoView-success assertion updated to expect the new `expected_bbox` argument.

  **Caught a real, pre-existing test-suite bug while confirming the fix against the full suite, before it ever reached the user**: adding those 4 new tests made `tests/test_focus_element_via_uia.py`'s FINAL test the one installing fake `uiautomation`/`win32gui` modules into `sys.modules` last — and that file never restored the originals afterward. On a machine where the real packages genuinely import (this dev sandbox has both installed), that leftover fake state silently leaked into whatever test file happened to run next in the same pytest process and did its own bare `import uiautomation` — `tests/test_focus_first_empty_field_active_tab.py::test_min_y_floor_skips_the_uia_search_entirely` started failing, but ONLY when run after the UIA test file, never in isolation — a real, if latent, test-isolation gap that existed before tonight and just happened to finally get exposed by having new tests be the "last one to leak." Fixed with an autouse `pytest.fixture` in `test_focus_element_via_uia.py` that snapshots and restores both `sys.modules` entries around every test in the file, so nothing it fakes can ever escape into another file's tests again. Full suite 420 passed / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures. Not yet re-verified live.

  **Re-verified live, next session, direct user report: "Check recent logs, you were doing so well until the Clear All modal."** Log confirmed: right after a failed 'Payment Frequency' dropdown-open click (a field near the bottom of its tab, close to the fixed footer button row), a 'Clear All' confirmation dialog (class `#32770` — the real Windows dialog class, not a misidentified sibling window) turned up open on the very next step, with no other click logged in between.

  **Root cause: a structural gap in the destructive-button guard's own coverage, not a new kind of bug.** `_find_destructive_button_at` (added earlier this session specifically for this exact incident class) checks `_snap2` — the transformer's raw, unverified predicted click position — before it gets used as a click target. But `_snap2` is a SHARED variable, reused by TWO separate branches: the navigate branch (which got the guard) and the "CLICK on empty combobox → treat as FILL" branch a few hundred lines above it (which did not). Both branches click the identical unverified position; only one was ever protected. Given 'Payment Frequency' sits close to the footer row, this is exactly the same class of incident the guard already exists to prevent — just reachable through the one door that was never locked.

  **Fixed by extending the exact same guard to the second branch**, mirroring the navigate branch's own pattern precisely (check `_snap2` against `_find_destructive_button_at` before clicking; Tab instead and move on if it lands on any button-type element). No new mechanism — the fix already existed and was already proven correct; it just needed to cover the sibling call site that shares its risky input. `_find_destructive_button_at`'s own docstring updated to describe both call sites instead of claiming it's navigate-only.

  Regression tests: `tests/test_destructive_button_guard.py` extended with `TestComboboxOpenBranchAlsoRefusesAnyButtonClick` (2 new tests — Tabs instead of opening a dropdown that would land on 'Clear All'; still opens a normal combobox dropdown when the position is safe). Full suite 422 passed / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures. Not yet re-verified live.

  **Re-verified live immediately after, direct user report: "Check most recent logs, skipped most fields in Vehicle after Current Mileage, what the fuck."** Cross-checked `logs/latest.log` against `car_insurance_form_wx.py`'s own authoritative field order (`"Current Mileage" → "Annual Miles Est." → "Primary Use" → "Garaging Location" → "Purchase Date"`) rather than guessing which fields were meant — confirmed real: `'Annual Miles Est.'` never appears ANYWHERE in the entire log (not even a blank-field lookup attempt), `'Primary Use'` was targeted by the low-confidence fallback but got `no_change` (nothing happened), and `'Garaging Location'` was the scroll's own tracked target (successfully revealed) but the very next action landed on `'Purchase Date'` instead — three consecutive fields skipped between two real fills. Run was user-interrupted at step 54, so this is a partial trace, not a full picture.

  **No fix shipped — added a targeted diagnostic instead of guessing a root cause the log doesn't actually support.** The one concrete fact the log gives: right where `'Annual Miles Est.'` should sit by field order, a plain navigate click executed and the validator confirmed `"Focus moved after click"` — but the NEXT step never logged `"LLM focused-field lookup"` for it, meaning the OPT2 fast path's `_t_is_type` check (whichever field WAS focused, run through `editcontrol/combobox/checkbox` type + empty + not-already-attempted) evaluated False. Two live-plausible explanations, genuinely indistinguishable from this log alone: (a) the click didn't actually land on `'Annual Miles Est.'`'s real input control (missed it, hit a label or the wrong nearby widget), or (b) `focused_element_id` didn't match anything in the freshly re-scanned `state["elements"]` — the same element-id-churn bug class already fixed twice this session for `state_validator.py` and `agent.py`'s own `_attempt_key`, just possibly resurfacing in this THIRD place (the plain focused-element lookup at the top of the OPT2 branch). Guessing between these without evidence risks the exact mistake this project's whole methodology exists to avoid.

  Added `[FOCUS-DIAG]` logging (no behavior change): whenever something was focused after a navigate click but doesn't get treated as fillable (and it isn't a checkbox, which already has its own explained reason via the TogglePattern fix from earlier tonight), logs either `"focused_element_id not found among N scanned elements"` (the churn signature) or the found element's own type/value/attempted flags — so the next live run gives a direct, cheap answer instead of another guess. Regression tests: `tests/test_focus_diagnostic_logging.py` (new, 6 tests — mirrors the exact condition for both the id-churn and found-but-excluded cases, confirms it stays silent for the normal successful path, for nothing-focused, and for the already-explained checkbox case). Full suite 428 passed / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures. **Needs one more live run before the real fix can be identified** — matches this project's own established pattern (used successfully several times earlier tonight) of diagnosing before guessing when the log genuinely doesn't say enough.

  **Re-verified live immediately after, direct user report: "It submitted but were we missing any fields? We're improving."** The run genuinely was better — cleaner overall, and the `[FOCUS-DIAG]` diagnostic added moments earlier fired constantly but showed EVERY single hit was the normal, benign case (an already-correctly-filled field being passed over, real value present, `already_attempted=True`) — confirming the diagnostic itself works correctly and isn't spamming false alarms. But cross-checking specifically for `'Annual Miles Est.'` (the field flagged missing last time) found it **still** never appears anywhere in this log either — and this time `[FOCUS-DIAG]` caught the mechanism directly: a plain navigate click aimed at it (its own bbox matched the click position) resulted in `[FOCUS-DIAG] focused 'Submit' type='buttoncontrol'` on the very next step — real focus landed on the Submit button instead, not the intended field.

  **Root cause, finally confirmed with direct evidence, not guessed.** `_record_attempt()` — the function that marks a field "attempted" so it's never offered as a target again — was always called with `state`, the STALE observation taken BEFORE the click executed, used only to build the prediction in the first place. For a click action, it looks up `self._elem_at(state, click_position)` — which, by construction, ALWAYS matches whatever element the click was AIMED at (since that position was itself derived by snapping to a candidate element in that same `state`). It never once checked what actually happened. The caller already takes a fresh `state_after` observation for validation a few lines below — it just was never threaded through to this call. So when the real click, for whatever OS/UIA-level reason, landed on the wrong control, the function still confidently marked the ORIGINALLY-INTENDED field "attempted" — permanently and silently excluding a field that was never actually focused or typed into. Same "verify what actually happened instead of assuming the intended target was hit" lesson this project has hit and fixed multiple times before (verify-at-fill, the redirect-verify fix), just never applied to this specific piece of bookkeeping until now.

  **Fixed by threading `state_after` through and preferring its verified `focused_element_id` for CLICK actions.** Falls back to the old position-based lookup only when `state_after` has nothing focused or wasn't provided (fully backward compatible). Deliberately left the KEYBOARD/type branch unchanged — a type action can legitimately trail a commit keystroke within the same step (e.g. a combobox selection), which could leave `state_after` focused on the NEXT field rather than the one just typed into, so the pre-action `state`'s focused element stays the more reliable signal there; this fix is scoped exactly to where the evidence pointed, not generalized further than the log actually supports.

  Regression tests: `tests/test_record_attempt_verifies_actual_focus.py` (new, 6 tests — marks the actually-focused element instead of the intended click target when they disagree, reproducing the exact 'Annual Miles Est.'/'Submit' scenario; the normal case where the click lands correctly is unaffected; both fallback paths when `state_after` has nothing useful; keyboard actions confirmed unaffected even when `state_after` shows a different field focused). Full suite 433 passed / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures. Not yet re-verified live.

The check's own comment claimed "already confirmed blank earlier this record," but `_attempted_keys` only ever means "something touched this element," not "we looked it up and it's genuinely empty" — exactly the distinction `self._leave_blank_keys` already exists to make elsewhere in this file (the OPT2 fill-gate's own `_fe2_confirmed_blank` check), just never applied to this specific combobox skip-check.

Lookups were never the cost. Confirmed via `AskUserQuestion` what "wasting steps" actually meant: nearly every field in tonight's logs took **two** separate steps — one step for a plain navigate click to focus the field, a second step for the next iteration's own top-of-loop check to notice it's now focused-and-empty and actually type into it.

  **Implemented a narrowly-scoped collapse**, not a broad restructuring of the loop. Right after a plain navigate click's post-action `state_after` is observed (a state the code already takes for validation — nothing new fetched), check whether the newly-focused element is an empty `editcontrol`/`input` with a known record value; if so, click its own bbox and type the value immediately, in the SAME step, instead of waiting for the next iteration to notice. Reassigning `prediction` to a `keyboard`/`text` shape and re-observing `state_after` means the existing verify-at-fill retry logic, `StateValidator`, and `_record_attempt` all run exactly as they already do for a normal type action — no separate verification path needed.

  Deliberately scoped narrow, not "collapse everything": only fires for a plain navigate click (`_is_plain_navigate_click`), never a redirect/combobox-open/checkbox click — those already have their own correct, more involved multi-step mechanics and forcing them through this same shortcut risked conflicting with already-proven-correct flows. Only `editcontrol`/`input` — combobox fills are their own open-dropdown-then-select sequence, not a simple collapse candidate. Only when a real value is found — a genuinely blank field is left for the next step's own established blank-handling logic (`_leave_blank_keys`, the fix earlier this session) rather than duplicating that logic here. Also fixed a subtlety while implementing: `_record_attempt()`'s keyboard branch reads the focused element from the state captured *before* the step began (deliberately, for the normal case — see that function's own docstring from earlier tonight) — but here the target field only became focused *during* this same step, so that lookup would find the wrong (previous) field. Marks the real target directly instead of relying on that downstream call for this one case.

- [x] `execution_hotkey_allowlist` — **Direct user report, severity well beyond a UX bug: "It closed the fucking Terminal holy shit."** The live process died silently mid-run — no traceback, no `"Run interrupted by user"` line — the log simply stops after a completely normal, successful step. Confirmed directly with the user: only the terminal closed, the form itself was unaffected — pointing at something reaching the terminal specifically, not a full system crash.

  **Traced the mechanism end-to-end, not guessed at.** `agent.py`'s own LLM system prompt explicitly lists `["alt+f4"] closes` as example hotkey vocabulary in the action-rules text sent to the model. The `"hotkey"` action-type handler takes `llm_action.get("keys", [])` — whatever the LLM returned — completely unfiltered, and returns it straight through as the executor's own `keystrokes` list. `executor.py`'s `_press_key` then called `pyautogui.hotkey(*key.split("+"))` on any `"modifier+key"` string with zero filtering. So an LLM response following its own prompt's example vocabulary (in a confusing or stuck moment) could execute `alt+f4` verbatim, closing whatever window had OS focus at that instant — the terminal running this very process, per the user's own account, not the target form. A destructive-CLICK guard already existed for mouse actions (`_find_destructive_button_at`) on exactly this reasoning; nothing equivalent existed for the keyboard.

  **Fixed with an allowlist, not a blocklist** — the same lesson `_find_destructive_button_at`'s own history already taught this project once (a keyword/blocklist is a whack-a-mole trap that needs a new entry every time something new is dangerous; refusing everything not explicitly known-safe is far more robust). `executor.py`'s `_press_key` now only executes a hotkey combo if it's in `_SAFE_HOTKEY_COMBOS` (`ctrl+a`, `ctrl+c`, `ctrl+v`, `ctrl+x`, `ctrl+z`, `shift+tab`) — anything else is refused and logged, never reaching `pyautogui.hotkey` at all. Also fixed the actual root — the prompt itself was teaching the model that `alt+f4` was valid vocabulary; rewrote it to explicitly list only the combos that are actually ever executed and to explicitly forbid window-closing combos for any reason, directing the model to `["tab"]` instead.

Full suite 466 passed / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures. Not yet re-verified live — and given the severity, this one is worth testing cautiously.

No keyboard action anywhere near any of them. Consistent with Windows' `CTRL_CLOSE_EVENT` default behavior: closing a console window immediately terminates any attached process with no handler for it, with nothing more flushed — which is exactly what a bare, unguarded `pyautogui.click()` landing on the terminal's own window would trigger.

It has no way to know a point lands in a completely different window, because that window's controls were never scanned at all. The sibling navigate-click branch a few hundred lines below already had exactly the missing protection — `self._point_in_form(_snap2, state)`, checked against the live `win32gui.GetWindowRect` of the locked form window — added earlier for a different incident. **Yet another coverage gap found live, 2026-08-13, direct report** ("Another error, it pressed the Clear All button, why did it do that"). The checkbox type-intercept's own uncheck-branch fallback redirect (`agent.py` ~L3997) — a DIFFERENT branch than any of the ones above — built a raw click from `find_visible_empty_target(...)`'s result, without ever routing it through `_find_destructive_button_at`. Traced end-to-end from the real log: `'Custom Equipment / Mods'` (checkbox, expected `'NO'`) resolved a redirect coordinate that, by click time, was occupied by the real Clear All button (`'#32770'` dialog popped immediately after, elements 138→145). Direct instruction from the user mid-investigation: *"Don't add a deterministic fix for the Clear All fields"* — ruled out extending `_DESTRUCTIVE_KW` or adding any new keyword/label-specific rule.

**Audited every `find_visible_empty_target`/`find_scroll_target_element` call site in the file (14 total) for the same shape of gap, not just the one that broke.** Found the identical pattern — resolve a target, try `_focus_element_via_uia` (safe, name-based) first, fall through to a raw coordinate click on failure, with no button check — at **7 sites total**: the checkbox-redirect that broke, plus 6 more (a stuck-pointer redirect, a combobox-already-attempted redirect, a leave-blank redirect, a tab-click "still unfilled on this tab" redirect, a low-confidence-fallback redirect, and the give-up-counter escalation redirect). The other 7 `find_visible_empty_target` call sites were confirmed safe by direct read — either diagnostic-only (never click) or resolve via `_focus_element_via_uia` alone with no coordinate fallback to guard.

**Fixed all 7 identically**: `if (not self._focus_element_via_uia(label, expected_bbox=bbox)) and not _find_destructive_button_at(elements, pos): execute click`. Same existing guard, same reasoning, no new mechanism, no keyword list — directly honors the "don't add a deterministic fix" instruction, since the guard already blocks ANY button-type element uniformly regardless of label; this only extends its *coverage* to call sites that shared the risk but never got wired through it. `_find_destructive_button_at`'s own docstring is now stale about which branches call it (says "originally just the NAVIGATE branch... extended to the combobox-open-dropdown branch") — not updated to list all 9 call sites individually, left as a known minor doc-drift rather than editing a well-established comment block for a count that may grow again.

15 new regression tests (`tests/test_redirect_click_destructive_button_guard.py`): reproduces the real incident shape directly (a redirect target's bbox-center landing on Clear All), confirms all three other footer buttons are caught too (not re-narrowed to one label), confirms a genuine empty field is NOT blocked, confirms UIA-focus success skips the coordinate click (and therefore the guard) entirely, and a direct source-level check that all 7 sites actually call the real `_find_destructive_button_at` (not a re-implementation that could drift). Full suite: **761 passed, 9 skipped, 0 failed.** Not yet re-verified live.

- [x] `execution_bc_fidelity_tab_coverage_blind_spot` — **Fixed 2026-08-11, found while investigating `scope1_tab_order` ("Fix tab-visit order but make sure its not deterministic and its the model doing it").** Confirmed the tab-jump problem is a training-data problem, not code (see `scope1_tab_order`'s update above) — the only real lever is more/cleaner demonstration data. But tracing how tab coverage actually gets *measured* surfaced that the QA tooling meant to check this had real bugs making it structurally blind past the first 3 tabs, independent of the model question.

  **Two files, three separate real bugs.** `scripts/bc_fidelity.py`'s `_TAB_PREFIXES` (drives `_tab_of()`, used for `tab_coverage`/`agent_tabs`/`tab_order_match`) had a phantom `"d1_": "Driver 1"` entry — no such prefix exists anywhere in the real form (`car_insurance_entry/car_insurance_form_wx.py`, confirmed by direct read, not the older unused `car_insurance_form.py` mock which was checked by mistake first and caught before it corrupted anything) — the Policyholder tab IS the primary driver. `"d2_"`/`"d3_"` were mapped to two fictitious separate "Driver 2"/"Driver 3" tabs instead of the one real "Drivers" tab. `"disc_"` (Coverage's 8 discount checkboxes) and `"hist_"` (the entire History tab) were missing outright, silently falling into "Other." `scripts/recording_quality_gate.py`'s `CANONICAL_TAB_ORDER` had the same wrong 9-tab list. Both fixed to match the real 8: Policy, Policyholder, Vehicle, Coverage, Drivers, History, Claims, Payment.

  **The deeper bug: `_LABEL_TO_KEY`** (parses the human-readable intake `.txt` into the gold-standard reference) only had entries for Policy/Policyholder/Vehicle — Coverage/Drivers/History/Claims/Payment were never extractable from the intake file at all, meaning `gold_tabs` (the `tab_coverage` denominator) could never include them regardless of what an agent actually did. Extending this naively would have been wrong: three real label collisions exist where the identical label text means a different field depending on section — "First Name" etc. differ between `[ Driver 2 ]`, `[ Driver 3 ]`, and the Policyholder default; "City"/"State"/"ZIP Code" differ between Payment's `[ Billing Address ]` and the Policyholder default; "Total Premium ($)"/"Payment Frequency" differ between Payment's `[ Billing Summary ]` and Coverage's `[ Premium Summary ]` default. A flat dict can't represent "same label, different field" — fixed by making `_parse_intake_record()` track the current `[ Section ]` header while iterating lines and checking a new `_SECTION_OVERRIDES` table before the section-agnostic default.

  **Two more real value-cleaning bugs found and fixed in the same pass**, both pre-existing and affecting every tab, not just the new ones: the `[VERIFY]`-stripping regex only matched the bare literal, missing every real variant in the intake file (`[VERIFY before saving]`, `[VERIFY -- do not store]`, ...) and leaving annotation text stuck in the parsed gold value; inline `← NOTE: ...` data-entry comments the intake file uses were never stripped either. Both would have falsely mismatched a correct agent-typed value against a corrupted gold value.

  **Caught by my own verification before shipping**: a direct sanity-check run against the real intake file showed `disc_good_driver` coming back as the raw string `'YES (check)'` instead of `True` — the ~18 new Coverage/discount checkbox fields were never added to the parser's `bool_keys` set. Fixed before writing tests, not after.

  **Verified end-to-end**: `_parse_intake_record()` against the real intake file's record 1 now extracts 173 fields across all 8 tabs (up from ~89 across 3). Gold standard regenerated (`python scripts/bc_fidelity.py --set-reference-from-source data_entry_tasks/data_entry_intake.txt --record 1`, old reference backed up first) and scored against a synthetic perfect submission: `tab_coverage: 1.0`, all 8 tabs in `tabs_covered`. (A separate, pre-existing, unrelated quirk noticed during this check: `score_submission()` treats any `False` boolean value as "not filled," which now shows up more since ~30 more boolean fields exist — a deliberate-looking design choice that predates this fix, not touched here.)

  Regression tests: `tests/test_bc_fidelity_tab_coverage.py` (new, 21 tests) — `_TAB_PREFIXES` matches the real form structure with no phantom tabs; each of the three section-override collisions resolves correctly and doesn't bleed into the other section; the full real intake file extracts exactly the 8 real tab names; all new boolean fields parse as real booleans, not strings; both value-cleaning fixes strip real intake-file variants correctly without breaking plain values. Full suite (see commit) clean, no regressions.

  **Follow-on, same investigation — real, current recording-data state, not previously visible**: ran `scripts/backfill_actions.py` for real (previously only dry-run) against `data/demos/eight_Tabs` — 3,291/3,291 steps backfilled, 0 errors. Audited the 12 sessions recorded since the last 2026-08-09 junk audit for the same no-typing pattern: 9 had zero keyboard activity (same signature as before); the other 3 looked promising (real keystroke counts) but a direct check of their captured UI element labels showed why they weren't — one captured an off-target Firefox/YouTube session instead of the form (the same *category* of accidental-off-target-capture this project has hit before with screenshots, this time in actual training data), the other two have zero captured UI elements at all (empty/broken captures). All 12 archived alongside the original 13, leaving exactly the 4 previously-verified-clean sessions — **no net recording progress since 2026-08-09**, now precisely documented rather than assumed. First real (non-vacuous) quality-gate report on those 4 sessions: avg tabs covered 4.0/8, Submit rate 57%, transition-mapping accuracy 42.7% (target ≥90%), encoding ambiguity 14.01% (target <5%) — worse-looking than the old fake 100% numbers, but real, and this is what the next recording session needs to actually improve against.

- [x] `execution_emergency_stop_hotkey_error_diagnosability` — **Fixed 2026-08-11, found while checking a live run's log during the `scope1_tab_order`/`execution_bc_fidelity_tab_coverage_blind_spot` work above.** A freshly-started live run logged `EmergencyStop: RegisterHotKey(Ctrl+Alt+K) failed (err=0) -- the keyboard failsafe is NOT active this run.` — a real safety concern, since `safety_emergency_stop_and_foreground_lockout` exists specifically as the independent, last-resort layer for the "couldn't reclaim my own mouse" incident.

  **Root cause of THIS specific failure**: a separate, still-running `run_task.py` process (PID 29192, alive continuously since 15:22 that afternoon, confirmed via `Get-CimInstance Win32_Process`) had already registered the same global hotkey — Win32's `RegisterHotKey` only allows one process to hold a given combo system-wide, so the newer run's own registration call genuinely failed. This also explains an unrelated-seeming test failure from earlier the same day: `tests/test_emergency_stop_hotkey.py`'s real end-to-end test timed out because its own child process's hotkey registration lost the same race against this same long-lived PID. Confirmed the process, asked the user directly rather than touching it (a live/possibly-legitimate process is never mine to kill) — user identified it as stuck and closed it themselves.

  **Root cause of the MISLEADING `err=0`, a separate and more durable bug**: `ctypes.get_last_error()` only reflects the real Win32 last-error code when the DLL handle was constructed with `use_last_error=True`. `agent/emergency_stop.py`'s `_listen()` used the plain cached `ctypes.windll.user32` instead — that handle never updates `get_last_error()`, so this branch was *always* going to log `err=0` on any real failure, not just this one, making every future diagnosis of a failed arm useless. Verified directly, not assumed: reproduced a genuine `RegisterHotKey` failure (registering the identical combo twice in one process) both ways — the old plain-`windll` path reported `0`; a `ctypes.WinDLL("user32", use_last_error=True)` handle correctly reported `1409` (`ERROR_HOTKEY_ALREADY_REGISTERED`).

  **Fixed**: `_listen()` now constructs `user32 = ctypes.WinDLL("user32", use_last_error=True)` instead of using the shared `ctypes.windll.user32` instance — purely a diagnosability fix, doesn't change whether a hotkey conflict happens, only whether it's possible to tell why when it does.

  Regression tests: `tests/test_emergency_stop_hotkey.py` gained `TestRegisterHotKeyFailureIsDiagnosable` (3 tests) — locks down the old broken behavior as a documented fact (plain `ctypes.windll.user32` really does always report `0`), confirms the fix reports the real `1409` error code against the same reproduced failure, and a direct source-level guard (`use_last_error=True` must appear in `_listen()`'s source) so a future refactor can't silently regress this.

  **Confirmed correct end-to-end, same day.** The user asked to close PID 29192 directly ("Close whatever that is") after it turned out to still be alive despite an earlier intent to close it — confirmed via `Get-CimInstance` it was still the identical process before touching it, then `Stop-Process -Id 29192 -Force` (exact PID, not a name-based kill — this project has a documented history of a name-based `Stop-Process -Force` accidentally killing an unrelated Notepad window; targeting the exact confirmed PID avoided repeating that). A fresh live run immediately before the kill had already independently proven the diagnosability fix itself: it logged the real `err=1409` (not the old `err=0`) for the exact same contention. After the kill, `tests/test_emergency_stop_hotkey.py`'s real end-to-end test — the one that actually presses Ctrl+Alt+K via synthetic input against a spawned child process — passed clean: **4 passed**, confirming the failsafe now arms and force-kills correctly with no contention.

  Same live-run session also gave the tab-coverage fix its first real, non-synthetic confirmation: the user ran `scripts/recording_quality_gate.py` themselves and got a genuine per-tab report (avg 7.0/8 tabs covered across the 4 clean sessions, up from the earlier 4.0/8 reading now that all 4 clean sessions are being read correctly) — real numbers, not the old vacuous defaults, on the user's own terminal.

- [x] `execution_root_directory_cleanup` — **2026-08-15, direct request** ("look at the root directory of our codebase with so many .py scripts, let's not do that and organize them under the scripts folder but keep the main most frequent script in the root directory"). 15 `.py` files sat at repo root; `run_task.py` (the actual live-run entry point, referenced constantly all session, the one script `run_task.py`'s own status bar and every log tonight is named after) is unambiguously "the main most frequent script" — kept in place. The other 14 (`train.py`, `build_capsule.py`, `demo_recorder.py`, `record_trace.py`, `replicate.py`, `run_agent.py`, `run_transformer.py`, plus the video/trace diagnostics `analyze_video.py`, `generate_trace.py`, `generate_two_state_trace.py`, `generate_s1_s3_trace.py`, `inspect_diff.py`, `inspect_text_changes.py`, `visualize_trace.py`) moved into `scripts/`.

  **Real breakage found and fixed, not assumed safe**: every moved file that resolves its own repo root via `os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__))` (or the equivalent inline `os.path.dirname(__file__)` in the four video/trace scripts) was silently computing `scripts/` instead of the real root the moment it moved — a wrong root breaks every path built from it (`.env`, `components/`, `data/`, model checkpoints) without raising an obvious error. Matched the fix to the convention `scripts/objectives_report.py` already established (`Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent`) — added one more `os.path.dirname(...)`/`.parent` to each of the 10 affected files, then verified by actually evaluating each computed root against the real one (not just reading the diff) — all 10 confirmed correct.

  Also fixed: every `python <name>.py` usage instruction across the 14 files (several cross-reference each other, e.g. `demo_recorder.py`'s own docstring pointing at `record_trace.py`) and the one current-usage mention in this file's own Quick Start / pipeline diagram — historical incident entries elsewhere in this file that happen to mention one of these filenames were left untouched, same "don't rewrite the audit trail" principle as the `car_insurance_entry` move. `run_agent.py` — found to be a genuine second, older, more generic `LLMAgent` entry point (no `INSURANCE_SCOPE`/`disable_transformer` wiring, unlike `run_task.py`) rather than dead code — moved, not deleted, and noted honestly in the Repository Layout as older/more general.

  No test imports any of the 14 by module name (checked directly, not assumed) — the only risk was the path-resolution bug above, now fixed and verified. Full suite: 1157 passed, 9 skipped, 0 failed — identical to before the move.

### Evaluation
Objectives 11 & 12 (thesis): quantifiable (10-20%+) and statistically significant
improvement over traditional RPA tools on setup time, adaptability, execution
error, cognitive workload.

- [x] `evaluation_bc_score_bug_fixed` — BC fidelity scorer was scoring stale submissions: `score_run()` picked "newest by mtime" in `data/output/submissions/`, which also receives unrelated auto-saves from the standalone form app — a run that crashed at step 1 still got a plausible BC score off a leftover blank submission. Fixed 2026-08-06: refuses to score anything older than the run's own start time (`agent._run_start_ts`).
- [ ] `evaluation_rpa_comparison` — RPA baseline comparison (setup time, adaptability, execution error, cognitive-workload proxy via intervention rate) with real significance testing. **Tooling verified functional 2026-08-12**: `scripts/compare_baseline.py` smoke-tested end-to-end against synthetic dummy data (both Intern-side and RPA-side JSON), produced correct per-dimension % improvement + Welch's t-test significance output with no errors. Genuinely blocked on real data collection, not on the tool: comparing against a traditional RPA tool means actually running that tool through the same insurance-form task multiple times and timing it — a live GUI-automation task in the same category as `run_task.py`, reserved for the user to run, not something this script (or any other automated process here) can substitute for or fabricate. *requires: `execution_agentic_integration_85`, `execution_error_rate_10`*
- [ ] `evaluation_objectives_dashboard` — Unified dashboard pulling every metric source into one pass/fail view per objective (`scripts/objectives_report.py`, built 2026-08-06) — exists and runs, but most underlying metrics still show "no data" pending real runs.
- [ ] `evaluation_benchmark` — Benchmark: compare against similar systems.
- [ ] `evaluation_chess_fidelity` — Chess fidelity benchmark: clone a human's play (openings, tendencies, time management, blunders); clone-ELO ≈ human-ELO + matching style.
- [ ] `evaluation_thesis_writing` — Thesis: Chapters 1-3 done; Chapter 4 (Results/Discussion) drafted but stale (see `evaluation_thesis_ch4_stale`); Chapter 5 (Summary/Conclusions/Recommendations) is empty headers only, not started.
- [ ] `evaluation_thesis_ch4_stale` — `Thesis.docx` Chapter 4's numbers (59 training runs, 43 end-to-end runs, 5,541 states, 60-78% field correctness, scalability objective marked not-fulfilled, etc.) are a snapshot from the *old* Intern — the pre-fix system, before this session's Submit unification/tab-focus/modal-dialog fixes and before the current ~50,000-step recording campaign. Decision: leave Ch.4 as-is (don't touch prose) until the new campaign is recorded and the model is retrained on it, then regenerate every cited number from the actual metrics scripts (`bc_fidelity.py`, `validate_transitions.py`, `encoding_ambiguity.py`, `objectives_report.py`) against the new data — do not hand-edit the numbers without a fresh run backing them. *requires: `scope1_rerecord_50k`*
- [ ] `evaluation_data_collection_500k` — Data collection: 500,000 traces in a convenience-sampling setting.
- [ ] `evaluation_transformer_ablation_test` — **Built 2026-08-14, direct request ("I have to verify that if we don't have the model we're using right now the performance would drop") after a real architectural tension surfaced the same night: two full, correctly-completed 176-field live runs both logged 0 real transformer calls.** `execution_opt2_fast_fill_known_values`'s deterministic lookup/batch/confirmed-blank paths had grown to cover the entire practice form — meaning the model wasn't proven unnecessary, it just hadn't been asked. A benchmark the model never touches can't validate or invalidate anything about it; this exists to make that a real, run-once test instead of an inference from reading logs.

  New `disable_transformer` flag on `LLMAgent` (`run_task.py --disable_transformer`, off by default — normal runs are completely unaffected). When set, `_predict()` never calls the real model at all: it returns a synthetic zero-confidence result instead of a real prediction. Every key in that result already has a `.get(key, default)` on the reading side elsewhere in `agent.py` (checked every real call site before writing this, not assumed), so the synthetic result routes through the exact same low-confidence Tab-fallback path the real model already triggers whenever ITS OWN confidence is low (`_gate_low_confidence_click`, `"[OPT2] pointer low-confidence ... Tab fallback"`) — not a new fallback invented for this test, the existing one just forced to fire on every step instead of rarely. Each skip is logged (`[ABLATION] transformer call #N skipped`) and counted (`self._ablation_transformer_calls_skipped`), reported in a summary line at the end of the run.

  The actual experiment this enables: run the same record twice — once normally, once with `--disable_transformer` — and compare. Does it still complete? Same fields, same values? Faster, slower, or the same? If both runs come out identical, that's hard proof the model isn't contributing anything on this specific form right now. If the ablation run gets stuck or fills something wrong that the normal run gets right, that pinpoints exactly where the model was doing real work that the deterministic paths don't cover.

  11 new tests (`tests/test_ablation_disable_transformer.py`): confirms the flag defaults to off, the real model is genuinely never reached (would raise/hang trying to load a nonexistent checkpoint in the test environment — succeeding proves it was skipped), the synthetic result has every key downstream code reads, the skip counter increments correctly, normal mode's code path is completely untouched (source-level check that the skip gate sits before the real import), and the CLI flag is correctly wired through to `LLMAgent` construction. Full suite: 1009 passed, 9 skipped, 1 failed (`tests/test_ghost_overlay.py`'s real-window lifecycle test — confirmed unrelated: passes cleanly in isolation, times out only under full-suite load, a pre-existing environment flake, not a regression from this change).

  **Not yet run** — per this project's standing rule, only the user runs live comparisons; this entry documents the tool, not a result. *requires a live run to close.*

### UI/UX
Not a thesis objective — developer tooling. The demo recorder's interface.

- [x] `uiux_electron_scaffold` — Electron app scaffolded and launches correctly (`app_electron/`): orange/white theme, `main.js`/`preload.js`/renderer, talks to the existing Python `DemoRecorder` over a stdio JSON bridge (`app/recorder_bridge.py`) since the recording logic (pynput/UIA/win32) has to stay in Python. Fixed an `ELECTRON_RUN_AS_NODE` env var forcing plain-Node mode instead of the real app. Started and finished scaffold 2026-08-06.
- [ ] `uiux_electron_feature_parity` — **Attempted 2026-08-10, mostly reverted per direct follow-up decisions; two real, separate bugs found and fixed along the way instead. `.exe` packaging still open.** Built a Screen Observer panel (mirroring `app/main.py`'s time-based `ObserverPanel`) and an F8 replay-newest hotkey + Replay ×N button (mirroring `DemoPanel`), both wired into `recorder_bridge.py` and the Electron renderer, smoke-tested end-to-end. **Both then explicitly reverted per direct user instruction** ("Remove the Screen Observer," then "Remove the Replay ×N and the F8 thing") — `app/main.py`'s Tkinter version is untouched; the Electron recorder panel is back to just Start/Stop, matching its original scope before tonight.

  While verifying "launch the app" was actually impossible as claimed: it wasn't. `EnumWindows` showed **zero windows** from every launch attempt tonight — first assumed to mean no interactive desktop at all. Checked properly instead of repeating the claim: `GetProcessWindowStation`/`GetThreadDesktop` showed a normal, interactive `WinSta0`/`Default` (`WSF_VISIBLE=1`), and VS Code's own Electron window was visibly open in the same session — proving desktop access was never the blocker. Root cause, found by running `electron.exe` synchronously and reading the actual crash: `TypeError: Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'whenReady')` — `ELECTRON_RUN_AS_NODE=1` was set in this shell's environment, forcing `electron.exe` to run as plain Node (`require('electron').app` returns `undefined` in that mode) instead of launching the real GUI runtime. Same class of bug `uiux_electron_scaffold` already fixed once, in a different place — env var leakage forcing Node-mode, not a code bug. Unsetting it for just the launch command (`env -u ELECTRON_RUN_AS_NODE`) produced a real, visible "Intern" window immediately — the first time this app had ever actually been seen running. The separate Python/wx `car_insurance_form_wx.py`/`demo_recorder.py` launch failures earlier the same night were re-attempted later, after killing several stray leftover processes, and launched fine — whatever the earlier blocker was (stale process contention is the leading guess, not confirmed) resolved itself; not chased further since it's no longer reproducing.

  **With the app finally visible and interactive for the first time, the user found three real, live bugs in sequence** — all pre-existing, never caught before because nobody could see this UI run until tonight:
  1. **"Stop is not working."** Traced to `DemoRecorder.run()` (`components/recorder/recorder.py`): its first real statement was `self._quit_event = threading.Event()` — replacing the event object `__init__` already created with a brand-new one. If `stop()` (bridge or Tkinter) called `.set()` on the old event in the narrow window before `run()` reached that line, the signal landed on an object nobody was waiting on anymore, and `run()` blocked on the new, still-unset event forever. Checked every real caller (`app/main.py`, `recorder_bridge.py`, `record_trace.py`) — all three construct a fresh `DemoRecorder` right before calling `.run()` exactly once, so the reassignment was never actually necessary. Fixed by removing it.
  2. **"Frames are still delayed, not real-time."** Traced to intentional design, not a bug, but a real UX gap: individual keystrokes accumulate silently in `_pending_text`/`_pending_keys` with zero queue push — a step only commits when a field is left (Tab/Enter/click elsewhere). That's correct and load-bearing (every trained checkpoint's data assumes one step per committed field, not per keystroke — changing it would degrade training data, the exact lesson learned earlier tonight). Left the recording semantics alone; instead, `recorder_bridge.py`'s `_poll()` now also reports whether there's live pending input, so the Electron UI can show a "typing…" indicator without changing what actually gets saved.
  3. **"Stuck on 3 frames," then Stop itself stopped working too.** Live log showed 3 real steps captured, each with `[!empty state]` (the snapshot subprocess wasn't answering from step 0000), then `"Flushing 58 pending action(s)…"` printed and nothing after — the whole app went unresponsive. Confirmed genuinely hung, not just slow: CPU-delta samples on every relevant process (the bridge, the snapshot subprocess) came back ~0 over 3s — idle, not busy. Root cause in `DemoRecorder._request_snapshot()`: `_req_q` is a bounded `multiprocessing.Queue` (`maxsize=4`); `_req_q.put(action_type or 1)` had no timeout, so once the subprocess stopped draining it — for any reason — every request after the 4th blocked forever, taking the entire worker thread down permanently (and Stop's own drain loop calls this same method, so Stop could never finish either). Fixed by giving `put()` the same timeout `get()` already had.

     **Re-verified live immediately after — deadlock gone (a 42-step session flushed and saved cleanly this time), but every single one of the 42 steps still showed `[!empty state]`, and the user reported it was "still not real time, so much delay."** Didn't accept "fixed" and move on — profiled `UIAutomationObserver.snapshot()` directly (`cProfile`, standalone, zero other load): **0.9-1.2s per call**, essentially all of it spread across ~13 separate UIA/COM property calls per element (`ControlTypeName`, `Name`, `BoundingRectangle`, `GetPattern` ×3 for Value/Toggle/Text fallbacks, etc.) — inherent to the `uiautomation` library's per-property-call design, not a bug in this codebase. Checked one specific lead (`CreateControlFromElement` called 206 times for only 81 elements) in case it was a real duplicate-call bug in `_walk` — it wasn't; that's the library's own internal cost of pattern lookups, not redundant work at the call-site level. A real speed fix means adopting UIA's cache-request batching API (one COM call for many properties instead of ~13) — a substantial rewrite, not attempted tonight without the ability to live-test it.

     What *was* fixable safely: the old 2.0s timeout left almost no margin above the 1.2s baseline — any real load (this repo syncs live via OneDrive; a concurrent process; anything) tips a marginal snapshot over the timeout, and the request silently comes back as `{}`, recorded as a real, empty-state step. Every step in the 42-step session showed exactly this. Raised the timeout 2.0s → 4.0s — doesn't make snapshots faster, but stops turning "slightly slow" into "silently wrong, empty data saved," which matters more for training data than the wait.

  **Direct user pushback, correctly: "Why is the recorder good on `main.py`?"** — a real, verified side-by-side comparison (a full Policy-tab pass, no typing, ~250 real frames on `main.py`). Same `DemoRecorder` class, same `_snapshot_proc` subprocess mechanism either way — if one host is reliably good and the other isn't, the difference has to be *how* it's hosted, not something inherent to UIA. Tested that directly instead of re-guessing: ran the exact subprocess IPC path outside Electron entirely (plain foreground process) — worked, real data, 0.8-2.1s/call. Ran it again with a hidden console window (matching Electron's `windowsHide: true`) — 5-10x faster (0.13-0.23s/call), but the *first* call came back empty. Neither test conclusively explained the live pattern, and a methodology flaw surfaced along the way: `UIAutomationObserver.snapshot()` has no concept of a locked target window — it captures whatever `win32gui.GetForegroundWindow()` returns at that instant, so an isolated test script has no guarantee it's even looking at the form.

  **Stopped guessing from isolated scripts and added targeted live diagnostics instead** (`[EMPTY-DIAG]` logging `app`/`window_title` on every empty step; `[SNAP-DIAG]` distinguishing `put()` timeout / `get()` timeout / a genuinely-empty-but-real response). Live evidence, re-tested twice: **every single request timed out** — `put() failed: Full()` once the bounded request queue backed up, and critically, `get() timed out: Empty()` on the *first two requests of the session, before the user had clicked anything at all*. CPU-delta sampling on the actual snapshot subprocess process confirmed it wasn't slow, it was **frozen — 0% CPU** while `subprocess_alive=True`. Correlated with the wx form's own window and process vanishing mid-session (no crash traceback, no Windows Event Log entry — a clean but unexplained exit) — the working theory: a UIA call was in-flight against the form's window at the moment it died, and that call never returns because its target no longer exists, permanently wedging the subprocess's single request-handling loop for the rest of the session. The deeper "why does the form keep vanishing" question was not resolved.

  **Decision: stop trying to fix cross-process IPC that had now failed in every tested configuration, and remove the layer instead of patching around it again.** `self._observer` — a `UIAutomationObserver` instance — is already constructed unconditionally in `__init__` regardless of subprocess mode, with its own comment documenting it as "~10x faster" than the general approach (foreground-window + Notepad only, not every visible window). Set `_use_subprocess = False` by default, routing every `_request_snapshot()` call through `self._observer.snapshot()` directly — no multiprocessing, no queues, no cross-process IPC to fail. Verified immediately, live: **0.03-0.17s per call, real data every time**, vs 0.8-2.1s (or permanently frozen) via the subprocess. Regression tests: `tests/test_recorder_subprocess_disabled_by_default.py` (5 tests — confirms the default, confirms `self._observer` still exists so the fallback path is safe, confirms `_request_snapshot` never touches `_req_q`/`_res_q` in this mode).

  **The full test suite segfaulted on the very next verification run — Windows access violation, exit 139, inside `uiautomation.GetFocusedControl`, called from the `_worker` thread.** Did not report the fix as done with a crash sitting unexplained. Root cause, found immediately: this file already has a `DemoRecorder._init_com()` method whose own docstring says *"Initialize COM for the current thread — required for UIA calls. Each thread that touches uiautomation must call this or it crashes."* It was defined and **never called anywhere in the file** — orphaned, almost certainly written for an earlier threading model before the (now-removed) subprocess redesign made it moot, since each subprocess got its own naturally-isolated COM state for free. Taking the subprocess away put real UIA calls on `_worker`'s thread for the first time ever, with nothing initializing COM there. Fixed by calling `self._init_com()` as the first line of `_worker()`. A native access violation can't be safely triggered or caught in a unit test — instead verified the checkable contract: `tests/test_worker_thread_com_init.py` (2 tests — confirms `_init_com()` runs before any snapshot request, and confirms a real `_worker` thread genuinely calls it, not just a re-implementation of the intent).

  **Re-ran the full suite after the COM fix: clean, 498 passed, no crash.** The suite is a meaningfully harsher test than a single live session (many `DemoRecorder` instances constructed across test files, increasing thread/COM contention) — passing it cleanly is real evidence, not just "worked once." True real-time responsiveness was the original ask; this turned out to be the actual blocker, not a speed-tuning problem.

  **Splash screen redesigned per direct request** ("looks very bad... painful in the eyes... add a simple and cute animation") — replaced the full-bleed saturated orange gradient + hard pulsing ring + sliding progress bar with a soft pastel gradient, a gentle bounce-in + idle bob on the mark, and a simple 3-dot bounce loader. Scoped to the splash only; the rest of the app's orange branding (titlebar, buttons) was untouched since it wasn't part of the complaint.

Full suite **498 passed** / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures — the last run of the night with no crash.   **Immediate follow-up, reversing the previous call: "Add a proper flying-chip animation that's smooth and not clunky."** Not a contradiction of the earlier `AskUserQuestion` answer so much as a rejection of *how* the dropped version had been built, not the idea of an animation at all — so this time it's a real FLIP (First-Last-Invert-Play) implementation instead of the naive "read a rect, `requestAnimationFrame`, guess a translate+scale to a midpoint" version from two rounds ago. Diagnosed what specifically made the old one clunky: it cloned the *entire wide group-header row* (icon + name + session count, sized to the row's full width) and animated it toward the panel's vague center, so the clone's shape never matched either its start point (a whole row) or its end point (a 30px circular icon) — a shape mismatch reads as a jump-cut no matter how the motion itself is eased.

  Rebuilt as a proper FLIP: the clone is placed and sized at its **final** spot first — a 30px circle with the exact same background/font-size as the real `.pp-capsule-icon` bubble it's about to become — then given an *inverse* transform (`translate(dx,dy) scale(s)`) that makes it visually sit at the **start** position (the small emoji glyph inside the clicked group's header) and size. A forced layout flush (`void clone.offsetWidth`) commits that starting transform before anything else touches the element, then clearing the transform to identity lets a single CSS transition animate position and scale together as one motion — the pattern the browser can run smoothly on the compositor thread, unlike animating `left`/`top`/`width`/`height` directly (which is what the old version effectively did across two separately-triggered style writes). Eased with `cubic-bezier(.16,1,.3,1)` — a calm ease-out with no bounce/overshoot, chosen specifically because "smooth" was the ask, not "playful." Opacity fades in over the first 180ms (the clone starts invisible so there's no instant duplicate-emoji flash at the exact spot the real, still-visible list emoji already is) and stays fully visible through landing — no fade-out, since the moment it's removed is the same moment `loadCapsuleIntoSlot()` reveals the real icon in the exact same place, so the swap is imperceptible. The panel's arrival pulse (`flashPlaySlot()`, kept from the previous round) still fires the instant the chip lands, as a second, distinct "it arrived" cue layered on top of the flight itself.

  No JS test framework to regression-test a CSS transition against, same as the rest of this round — verified by re-reading the FLIP math by hand (start/end rect centers, the inverse-transform derivation) and live: Electron relaunched, clean single-window startup, no console errors. Full Python suite re-run as a sanity net even though this round touched only CSS/JS: 505 passed / 9 skipped, no crash (ran with the 2 pre-existing, unrelated Tesseract-dependent tests excluded rather than re-verified individually, since nothing in this round could plausibly affect OCR). The animation's actual look-and-feel in motion hasn't been confirmed by the user yet — that's a "does this feel right" check only they can make by actually clicking a group.

  **Same-turn follow-up, three more requests: gray out the placeholder emoji, replace typing an emoji with clicking one from a displayed set, and gray out session rows.** All UI-polish, addressed together since they touch the same handful of elements.

  **Emoji picker, not a text box.** Deleted the previous round's click→text-input→blur-to-commit flow (and its now-dead `.pp-capsule-emoji-input` CSS) entirely. Clicking the Play panel's capsule icon now opens a small fixed-position grid (`.emoji-picker`, anchored just under the bubble via its own `getBoundingClientRect()`) listing a curated `EMOJI_CHOICES` set plus the placeholder puzzle piece as an explicit "clear" tile — click any tile and it commits immediately via the existing `capsulesAPI.setEmoji()`, no separate confirm step. Closes on an outside click or Escape; the outside-click listener deliberately uses `mousedown` rather than `click` so it can't catch the very click that opened the picker (`mousedown` for that click already finished before the opening `click` handler even runs — a `mousedown` listener registered inside that handler can only ever fire on a later, separate click).

  **Gray "template" emoji, with a real technical caveat worth recording:** an unset emoji now shows in gray chrome — `var(--chip-bg)` background, `var(--dim)` text/border — on the Play panel's icon, the flying clone, and each workflow group's header emoji, toggled via a shared `applyCapsuleEmojiDisplay()` helper and an `.is-placeholder` class. What this *can't* do: recolor the emoji glyph itself. Standard emoji characters render through the OS's own color emoji font (Segoe UI Emoji on Windows), which ignores the CSS `color` property entirely — "gray" here is necessarily the bubble/background around the glyph, never the glyph's own colors, on any platform. Worth knowing before anyone asks for a literal grayscale puzzle-piece icon later.

  **Session rows grayed**: `.wf-session .sname` changed from `var(--ink)` to `var(--dim)`, matching `.ssteps`'s existing color — consistent with the "sessions are reference data to skim, not the focal action" framing already established for this list two rounds ago.

- [x] `ghost_cursor_and_caret` — **Shipped 2026-08-10.** Direct request, unprompted by anything in the Electron work above: replace the *live agent's* real mouse-cursor hijacking with a decoupled, "Intern"-branded visual overlay ("ghost cursor" for clicks, "ghost caret" for typing) — orange main color, white overtones, slightly larger than a normal pointer, "Intern" labeled. This is `run_task.py`/`run_transformer.py`'s actual execution layer, not the Electron dev tool — the first change this session to the live agent's real click/type mechanism itself.

  **Investigated before building anything** (explicit request: "Look into that, please" before committing to an approach). Read `agent/executor.py` end-to-end: `_click()` was pure `pyautogui.moveTo()`+`.click()` — always moves the real OS cursor, no exceptions. But `agent.py` already had two *proven* precedents for cursor-free UIA interaction on this exact form: a working `SetFocus()` path (used live today) and a UIA-lookup `_btn.Click()` fallback for the Submit button. `ui_observer.py` already reads `ValuePattern`/`TogglePattern` via `ctrl.GetPattern(PatternId.X)` for STATE — the same generic call shape, aimed at *writing* instead, is what `InvokePattern.Invoke()`/`TogglePattern.Toggle()`/`SelectionItemPattern.Select()` need. Conclusion, not a guess: most clicks (buttons, checkboxes, tabs) can go fully cursor-free; comboboxes and non-standard controls likely can't reliably, so a coordinate-click fallback stays necessary — accurate most of the time, not a claimed 100%.

  **`agent/executor.py` — `_click()` now tries a real, cursor-free UIA action first.** New `_try_uia_invoke(x, y)`: `uiautomation.ControlFromPoint(x, y)` (confirmed via its own docstring — pure `IUIAutomation.ElementFromPoint`, no mouse involvement) to get the live control at that screen point, then tries `GetPattern(InvokePattern)` → `GetPattern(TogglePattern)` → `GetPattern(SelectionItemPattern)` in order, invoking whichever exists first. Only if none apply does `_click()` fall back to the original `pyautogui.moveTo()`+`.click()` — the real cursor only moves for that fallback minority now, not by default. `_keyboard()` gained `_show_ghost_caret()`, called at the top for every live keyboard action: reads `uiautomation.GetFocusedControl().BoundingRectangle` (the same attribute names `ui_observer.py` already reads elsewhere) and positions the ghost caret there — typing itself was already cursor-free (clipboard paste / `pyautogui.typewrite` never moved the mouse), so this half of the feature adds zero new interaction risk, purely a visualization.

  **New `agent/ghost_overlay.py` — `GhostOverlay`.** A transparent, always-on-top Tkinter window drawing the actual cursor/caret shapes, run on its own background thread (a thread-safe `queue.Queue` is the only contact surface — `show_cursor()`/`hide_cursor()`/`show_caret()`/`hide_caret()` are safe from any thread; the Tk thread drains the queue on its own `after()` timer, never touched directly by the agent thread). Cursor: a slightly-large arrow polygon, white drawn first and wider as an "overtone" halo, orange on top, with a small orange-and-white "Intern" label pill near the tip. Caret: an orange/white vertical bar at the focused field, blinking every 500ms, same label treatment. Both match the app's own `--orange`/`--orange-dark` palette from the Electron work above, for brand consistency across the whole project, not just this feature.

  **The one thing that had to be verified, not assumed, before this could ship**: a naive transparent overlay (Tkinter's `-transparentcolor` alone) only makes *color-keyed* pixels invisible — the OPAQUE pixels we actually draw (the cursor/caret shapes) are normal, clickable window content by default. Since those shapes are deliberately drawn exactly where a real click is about to land, an overlay without extra care would sit directly in front of the target control and silently swallow the click meant for it — turning a cosmetic feature into a correctness regression in the one part of this project that can least afford one. Fixed with the Win32 `WS_EX_TRANSPARENT` extended window style (`_make_click_through()`, raw `ctypes.windll.user32.SetWindowLongW`, OR'd in alongside the `WS_EX_LAYERED` `-transparentcolor` already requires) — it makes the *entire* window, drawn pixels included, invisible to mouse hit-testing while still rendering normally, so real clicks and the agent's own UIA calls both pass straight through unconditionally. Confirmed live in this exact environment before writing any tests around it (a standalone script: transparentcolor works, `SetWindowLongW` succeeds, clean mainloop exit) rather than assumed from documentation alone.

  **Opt-in, default OFF, specifically to protect the test suite**: `ActionExecutor.__init__` gained `ghost_cursor: bool = False`. Dozens of existing tests construct `ActionExecutor(dry_run=False)` directly with `pyautogui` mocked out — if a real overlay window had defaulted to spawning automatically, every one of those would have started silently popping up windows during `pytest tests/`. Only `agent.py`'s and `run_transformer.py`'s live-run construction sites pass `ghost_cursor=True` — the only two real live-execution entry points in this repo (checked; both generalize the same way, not hardcoded to `run_task.py` alone, per this project's own "let the Agent work throughout, don't hard-code for one task" rule).

  **A real crash was hit and root-caused while writing the tests, not shipped around** — worth recording as its own lesson: an early draft of `tests/test_ghost_overlay.py` gave each lifecycle scenario its own real `GhostOverlay` (several separate `tk.Tk()` roots created/destroyed across the file). Several tests later, in a completely unrelated test class, pytest hard-crashed with `Windows fatal exception: code 0x80000003` during plain `MagicMock` garbage collection. Root-caused, not guessed around: isolating the real-overlay test alone reproduced Tcl's own `Tcl_AsyncDelete: async handler deleted by the wrong thread` — a genuine, known Tcl/Tk limitation (interpreter teardown expects to happen on the thread that created it; a background-thread-owned Tk root being reaped later doesn't guarantee that). In isolation it's just scary-looking noise on exit; left running inside the shared ~500-test pytest process, later unrelated GC activity hit the corrupted state and hard-crashed the *entire* suite, not just the one test. Fixed by isolating that one test into its own subprocess (`subprocess.run([sys.executable, "-c", ...])`) — any Tcl/Tk teardown fallout is confined to a throwaway child process the OS fully reaps, never touching the parent pytest process's memory/GC state. A second, separate crash was hit and fixed the same way: an earlier draft mocked `ctypes.windll` directly via `patch.object` to test the click-through window style — this alone triggered the same class of fault, since `ctypes.windll` is a live, C-backed, process-wide singleton, not an ordinary attribute safe to swap mid-process. Fixed with dependency injection instead (`_make_click_through(root, user32=None)`, tests pass a plain `MagicMock` for `user32` and never touch the real `ctypes.windll`). Production is not exposed to either failure mode: a live run's `GhostOverlay` lives for the whole process and is torn down by the same process exit that would print the same harmless noise on its way out, never followed by further work that could crash on it.

  Regression tests: `tests/test_ghost_overlay.py` (10 tests — one real, subprocess-isolated end-to-end lifecycle test; the rest pure mocks for tkinter-unavailable fallback and the click-through window-style logic, none spawning a second real Tk root); `tests/test_executor_ghost_cursor.py` (22 tests — the opt-in-default-off guarantee explicitly checked with a mocked `GhostOverlay` class asserted never called; `_try_uia_invoke`'s full fallthrough chain (Invoke → Toggle → SelectionItem → coordinate fallback) and exception safety; the actual regression-critical check that a successful invoke skips `pyautogui.moveTo`/`.click()` entirely while a failed one falls back to the exact previous behavior; ghost-caret placement math against a mocked `BoundingRectangle`, including a degenerate zero-size rect producing no caret). Full suite: **545 passed** / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures, no crash — a meaningfully harsher run than the isolated files alone, confirming the subprocess isolation actually holds under the full suite's load. Not yet confirmed by the user: how the cursor/caret actually look and feel during a real live run — that's the user's call to trigger, same as every other live run in this project.

  **Immediate live bug report: "I pressed Play and it's not running at all." Plus a separate, related request: "add a countdown too."** Diagnosed from real code, not guessed — read `run_task.py` end-to-end and found its existing pre-run countdown (`print(f"  Starting in {i}...", end="\r")`, five lines, meant to give the user time to click into the target window) had **zero `\n` between any of its five lines** — `\r` is a terminal-only cursor trick, meaningless to `recorder_bridge.py`'s `run_capsule()`, which reads the subprocess's stdout via `for line in proc.stdout:` (Python's text-mode pipe iteration only yields once a real newline arrives). The entire 5-second countdown window was therefore **completely invisible** in the Play panel's Activity log — the process was running (or about to), the user just had nothing on screen telling them so, which reads exactly like "not running at all." A second, compounding issue found in the same pass: `recorder_bridge.py`'s `subprocess.Popen([sys.executable, run_task_script, ...])` never passed `-u` — a non-tty stdout is block-buffered by default in Python, the same class of problem this project already fixed once elsewhere for `car_insurance_form_wx.py`'s subprocess launch, just never carried over to this newer capsule-run path.

  **Fixed both, and turned the countdown into the requested feature instead of just patching around it.** `run_task.py`'s countdown was extracted into a standalone, testable `print_countdown(seconds=5, sleep_fn=None, print_fn=None)` (injectable params specifically so a test can run it without a real 5-second sleep) that prints structured, flushed, newline-terminated lines — `COUNTDOWN_BEGIN`, `COUNTDOWN 5` … `COUNTDOWN 1`, `COUNTDOWN_END` — instead of the old cursor trick. `recorder_bridge.py`'s `run_capsule()` gained `-u` in its `Popen` argument list, matching the existing precedent exactly. The Play panel's renderer now recognizes these markers (`handleCapsuleProgressLine()`) and renders an actual countdown UI — a big orange number in its own `#ppCountdown` panel, replacing the useless 5 seconds of silence — rather than just letting `COUNTDOWN 3` scroll by as ordinary log text. Deliberately a **plain in-place number with a brief scale/opacity pulse per tick, nothing that travels**, consistent with the still-fresh lesson from the fly-to-Play animation two commits ago (fast motion covering real screen distance reads as uncomfortable regardless of how well it's implemented; a calm, stationary pulse doesn't have that problem). `hideCountdown()` is also called defensively on `capsule_done`/`error`/a fresh `capsule_started`, so a run that crashes mid-countdown can't leave the banner stuck on screen.

  Regression tests: `tests/test_run_task_countdown.py` (6 tests) — the exact regression (every line is a separate string, never one `\r`-joined blob with embedded carriage returns), the begin/end markers, the correct 5→1 sequence, one real second of sleep per tick, a custom duration, and that the injectable params default to the real `time.sleep`/`print` when not overridden (so the test-friendly seam isn't a parallel, divergent implementation). `tests/test_recorder_bridge_capsule_run.py`'s existing Popen-args test extended to assert `"-u"` is the second argument. Full suite: **551 passed** / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures, no crash. Electron relaunched live with this build (single window, no startup errors).
